```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We systematically compare \cls{}, \cwm{}, and \traj{} across two model families, two complexity levels, and multiple seeds to characterize the formulation design space for neural code verification.
5	
6	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Datasets.}
10	\emph{Function-level}: mutated variants from HumanEval~\cite{chen2021humaneval} and MBPP~\cite{austin2021mbpp} (five mutation types), labels verified by execution. 19,076 samples (train 15,290 / val 1,896 / test 1,890; pass:fail $\approx$ 52:48, majority baseline 51.75\%).
11	\emph{Repo-level}: per-test instances from SWE-bench Verified~\cite{chowdhury2024swebenchverified} mutants. ${\sim}$70K training samples; val ($n{=}9{,}335$) and test ($n{=}9{,}784$) have zero problem overlap (majority baseline 72.96\%). Val-based checkpoint selection follows standard practice~\cite{jimenez2024swebench}.
12	
13	\paragraph{Models and training.}
14	We fine-tune Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3} with LoRA~\cite{hu2022lora} (rank 16, 3 epochs) across five seeds. Seeds 42/123/456 use bfloat16; seeds 789/0 use NF4 quantization~\cite{dettmers2024qlora}. For cross-model validation, we additionally fine-tune DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B~\cite{guo2024deepseekcoder} with identical LoRA configuration (2 seeds each for \cls{} and \cwm{}). Our \cwm{} baseline is a LoRA fine-tuned variant for controlled comparison, not a reproduction of the 32B Code World Model~\cite{meta2025cwm}. Repo-level models use val-selected checkpoints. Zero-shot baselines use Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-32B (NF4) as LLM-as-judge. Full hyperparameter details appear in Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}.
15	
16	\paragraph{Statistics.}
17	Confidence intervals use clustered bootstrap~\cite{davison1997bootstrap} (114 problem clusters, 10K resamples); per-test vs.\ trajectory-level comparison uses TOST~\cite{schuirmann1987tost} ($\pm$2\pp{} margin). ICC~\cite{shrout1979icc} quantifies within-problem correlation. Oracle analysis uses SWE-bench Verified (500 instances).
18	
19	
20	\subsection{Function-Level: Formulation Equivalence}
21	\label{sec:func_results}
22	
23	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports function-level results across formulations and model families.
24	
25	\begin{table}[t]
26	\centering
27	\small
28	\caption{Function-level per-test verification results on HumanEval+MBPP test set. \cls{}: per-test binary classification; \cwm{}: execution output generation; \traj{}: trajectory-level scoring; ZS: zero-shot LLM-as-judge. Mean $\pm$ std over seeds reported where applicable. Seeds 42/123/456 use full bfloat16; seeds 789/0 use NF4 quantization. \cls{} and \cwm{} are evaluated on the full 1,890 test samples; \traj{} is evaluated on a 1,032-sample subset covering the same 447 (problem, mutation) variants with representative test cases selected to fit the trajectory input format.}
29	\label{tab:main_results}
30	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
31	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
32	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
33	\toprule
34	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Model} & \textbf{Seeds} & \textbf{Accuracy (\%)} \\
35	\midrule
36	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\emph{Qwen3 (primary)}} \\
37	\cls{} (fine-tuned) & 4B & 5 & \textbf{88.43 $\pm$ 0.78} \\
38	\cwm{} (fine-tuned) & 4B & 5 & 87.19 $\pm$ 0.37 \\
39	\traj{} (fine-tuned) & 4B & 5 & 86.30 $\pm$ 0.97 \\
40	\midrule
41	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\emph{DeepSeek-Coder (cross-model validation)}} \\
42	\cls{} (fine-tuned) & 6.7B & 2 & 86.85 $\pm$ 0.11 \\
43	\cwm{} (fine-tuned) & 6.7B & 2 & 86.83 $\pm$ 1.49 \\
44	\traj{} (fine-tuned) & 6.7B & 2 & 84.16 $\pm$ 1.30 \\
45	\midrule
46	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\emph{Prompting baselines}} \\
47	0-shot & 8B (Qwen3) & 1 & 68.70 \\
48	0-shot & 6.7B (DeepSeek) & 1 & 63.00 \\
49	ZS LLM-as-judge & 8B (Qwen3) & 1 & 64.40 \\
50	ZS LLM-as-judge & 6.7B (DeepSeek) & 1 & 56.56 \\
51	ZS LLM-as-judge & 32B (NF4) & 1 & 71.69 \\
52	3-shot ICL & 8B (Qwen3) & 1 & 68.78 \\
53	3-shot ICL & 6.7B (DeepSeek) & 1 & 59.95 \\
54	\midrule
55	Majority (pass) & --- & --- & 51.75 \\
56	\bottomrule
57	\end{tabular}}
58	\end{table}
59	
60	All three formulations achieve comparable accuracy on Qwen3-4B: \cls{} 88.43\% ($\pm$0.78), \cwm{} 87.19\% ($\pm$0.37), \traj{} 86.30\% ($\pm$0.97). The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap of 1.24\pp{} is modest, within 1.6$\times$ of the seed standard deviation for \cls{}. DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B confirms this pattern across all three formulations: \cls{} 86.85\% ($\pm$0.11), \cwm{} 86.83\% ($\pm$1.49), \traj{} 84.16\% ($\pm$1.30). The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap of 0.02\pp{} is indistinguishable from zero; \traj{} is 2.69\pp{} below \cls{}, consistent with the Qwen3 ordering. At this complexity level, execution outputs are short and structured, generation-specific failure modes (truncation, format compliance) are not triggered, and formulation choice does not meaningfully affect accuracy. Under standard instruction-following prompting, baselines reveal three layers of the verification challenge. First, \emph{repo-level inherent difficulty}: 0-shot accuracy drops to 49.9\% (Qwen3-8B) and 48.5\% (DeepSeek-6.7B, 8.7\% unparseable) at repo level, below the majority baseline (72.96\%). Both models exhibit systematic but non-discriminative bias (Qwen3 predicts fail 75\% of the time; DeepSeek predicts pass 73\%), achieving ${\sim}$49\% accuracy in both cases. Three-shot ICL fails entirely at repo level (100\% unparseable due to context overflow). Second, \emph{fine-tuning necessity}: the 0-shot$\to$fine-tuned gain is larger at repo level (25--35\pp{}) than at function level (18--20\pp{}); repository-level verification requires task-specific training under standard prompting, not merely a capable base model. At function level, 0-shot achieves 68.70\% (Qwen3-8B) and 63.00\% (DeepSeek-6.7B); the LLM-as-judge variant is lower (64.40\%, 56.56\%); 3-shot ICL reaches 68.78\% and 59.95\%. Third, \emph{formulation choice}: even after fine-tuning, formulation selection matters at repo level (\cls{} outperforms \cwm{} by 13--26\pp{}), an interaction effect absent at function level where all formulations converge. \cwm{} produces 0\% unparseable outputs at function level for both model families.
61	
62	\paragraph{Variance asymmetry.} \cwm{}'s higher seed variance on DeepSeek ($\sigma{=}1.49$ vs.\ \cls{} $\sigma{=}0.11$) mirrors the Qwen3 pattern (\S\ref{sec:analysis}, paragraph d): generation is more sensitive to initialization than classification, consistent with the output space being less constrained.
63	
64	
65	\subsection{Scaling Behavior}
66	\label{sec:scaling}
67	
68	Table~\ref{tab:scaling} compares all three formulations at two model sizes using two matched seeds (42, 123), all trained in full bfloat16 precision to ensure a controlled comparison.
69

84	\cwm{} & 8B & 87.67 & 86.98 & 87.33 & \\

91	
92	\cls{} and \traj{} both scale positively from 4B to 8B (+1.27\pp{} and +1.70\pp{} respectively), while \cwm{} remains flat ($-$0.03\pp{}). At 8B, the ranking is \cls{} (89.94) $>$ \traj{} (87.70) $>$ \cwm{} (87.33). The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap widens from 1.32\pp{} at 4B to 2.62\pp{} at 8B (Figure~\ref{fig:scaling_gap}); clustered bootstrap on the 3-seed superset (42, 123, 456 for \cls{}/\cwm{}) yields $p = 0.0066$ (95\% CI $[1.80, 2.71]$), which confirms the widening is not a sampling artifact.
93	
94	\cwm{} is the only formulation that does not benefit from increased capacity under our experimental configuration (LoRA rank 16, fixed hyperparameters). The \cwm{} stagnation ($-$0.03\pp{}) falls within seed noise and could reflect suboptimal hyperparameter transfer at 8B rather than a fundamental scaling limitation. We do not claim that generation \emph{cannot} benefit from scale, only that it did not in this configuration. This pattern is observed within Qwen3 only; whether it generalizes across model families at function level is not yet tested. At repository-level complexity, the interaction between formulation and scale is examined in \S\ref{sec:repo}.
95	
96	
97	\subsection{Per-Test vs.\ Trajectory-Level Equivalence}
98	\label{sec:equivalence}
99	
100	We compare \cls{} (aggregated via all-pass rule) against \traj{} on 523 variant-level instances across five seeds. The pooled gap is $-$0.45\pp{} (95\% CI [$-$1.71, +0.84]); the TOST equivalence test confirms statistical equivalence at $p = 0.007$ ($\pm$2\pp{} margin), with a minimum equivalence margin of $\pm$1.63\pp{}. The $\pm$2\pp{} margin reflects practical significance: formulation differences below 2\pp{} are unlikely to affect deployment decisions where absolute accuracy dominates the cost-benefit analysis. Within-problem predictions are correlated (ICC $= 0.191$, $p < 0.0001$, DEFF $= 3.97$); all statistical tests use clustered bootstrap accordingly. On DeepSeek-Coder, the observed \cls{}--\cwm{} gap of 0.02\pp{} is consistent with equivalence, but with only 2 seeds the comparison is underpowered (TOST not significant even at $\pm$5\pp{} margin). Per-test classification provides diagnostic information (which specific tests fail) at no cost to variant-level accuracy, a result that parallels process supervision~\cite{lightman2023letsverify}, where step-level verification enables error localization without accuracy cost.
101	
102	\paragraph{TOST margin sensitivity.}
103	Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports equivalence test results across multiple margins. The observed mean difference is 1.24\pp{} (\cls{} $>$ \cwm{}), so the minimum margin at which equivalence holds is $\pm$1.63\pp{}. At $\pm$1\pp{}, the margin is too tight to contain the observed difference ($p = 0.871$); at $\pm$2\pp{}, equivalence is confirmed ($p = 0.007$); at $\pm$3\pp{}, evidence is overwhelming ($p < 0.001$). We adopt $\pm$2\pp{} as the primary margin because formulation differences below 2\pp{} are unlikely to affect deployment decisions: at this scale, factors such as seed selection, checkpoint choice, and hyperparameter tuning dominate the accuracy variance.
104	
105	\begin{table}[h]
106	\centering
107	\small
108	\caption{TOST equivalence sensitivity (Qwen3-4B, 5 seeds, $\alpha{=}0.05$). Observed mean \cls{}--\cwm{} difference: 1.24\pp{}.}
109	\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}
110	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{5pt}
111	\begin{tabular}{ccc}
112	\toprule
113	\textbf{Margin $\delta$ (\pp{})} & \textbf{TOST $p$} & \textbf{Equivalent?} \\
114	\midrule
115	$\pm$1 & 0.871 & No \\
116	$\pm$2 & 0.007 & Yes \\
117	$\pm$3 & $<$0.001 & Yes \\
118	\bottomrule
119	\end{tabular}
120	\end{table}
121	
122	\paragraph{\cls{}--\traj{} accuracy relationship.}
123	Across both model families, \cls{} achieves slightly higher accuracy than \traj{}: Qwen3-4B \cls{} 88.43\% vs.\ \traj{} 86.30\% (gap 2.13\pp{}); DeepSeek-6.7B \cls{} 86.85\% vs.\ \traj{} 84.16\% (gap 2.69\pp{}). The consistency of this ordering across models suggests that per-test decomposition provides a modest accuracy benefit beyond diagnostic granularity: processing each test independently allows the model to focus on a single pass/fail decision rather than integrating signals across an entire test trajectory. \cls{} additionally offers ${\sim}$24$\times$ per-instance speedup over \traj{} at function level (2.3 tests/instance $\times$ 47ms/call vs.\ 2,632ms single \traj{} call; DeepSeek-6.7B, RTX 5090). This advantage narrows at repo level (${\sim}$3$\times$; 12.3 tests $\times$ 70ms vs.\ 2,631ms) because \cls{} per-test cost grows linearly with test count. Combined with the diagnostic granularity advantage (\S\ref{sec:oracle-results}), per-test classification Pareto-dominates trajectory-level scoring on accuracy, efficiency, and informativeness at function-level complexity; at repo level the efficiency advantage is modest and disappears entirely for instances with $>$50 tests.
124	
125	
126	\subsection{Accuracy-Utility Frontier}
127	\label{sec:oracle-results}
128	
129	Oracle analysis on SWE-bench Verified (500 instances) reveals that per-test advantage concentrates sharply: at $T = 1$ F2P tests (68.7\% of instances), per-test and trajectory-level are informationally equivalent. The advantage materializes at $T \geq 2$ (31.3\%), with $T = 2$ alone contributing 55.3\% of the total 10.93\pp{} block-correlated advantage. Pass-to-pass (P2P) regression is negligible (1.04\% mean, 0\% median), so the practical value concentrates in fail-to-pass discrimination. Full stratification and the i.i.d.\ vs.\ block-correlated comparison appear in Appendix~\ref{app:oracle}.
130	
131	
132	\subsection{Repo-Level: Complexity-Dependent Divergence}
133	\label{sec:repo}
134	
135	Table~\ref{tab:repo_level} compares \cls{} and \cwm{} on SWE-bench Verified per-test classification at repository-level complexity. All checkpoints are selected by validation accuracy; the test set ($n{=}9{,}784$) has zero problem overlap with validation. We report both overall accuracy (where unparseable outputs default to the majority class, pass) and parseable-only accuracy to disentangle generation quality from default assignment effects.
136

141	\label{tab:repo_level}

161	
162	\paragraph{Formulation divergence.} At repository-level complexity, \cls{} and \cwm{} diverge sharply on Qwen3: the gap is 25.59\pp{} at 4B (\cls{} 83.63\% vs.\ \cwm{} 58.04\%, seed 42; Figure~\ref{fig:scaling_gap}) and 19.31\pp{} at 8B (84.48\% vs.\ 65.17\%). Both gaps are an order of magnitude larger than at function level (1--3\pp{}, Table~\ref{tab:scaling}); formulation effectiveness is complexity-dependent. \cwm{} falls below the majority baseline at both scales ($-$14.92\pp{} at 4B, $-$7.79\pp{} at 8B): negative utility for this model/formulation combination.
163	
164	\paragraph{Cross-model validation.} DeepSeek-Coder repo-level results (\cls{} final, \cwm{} preliminary) confirm the formulation-inherent nature of the divergence while revealing model-contingent magnitude. Both model families exhibit the same ordering (\cls{} $>$ \cwm{}), but the function$\to$repo degradation differs: Qwen3-8B degrades ${\sim}$5\pp{} for \cls{} and ${\sim}$22\pp{} for \cwm{}, while DeepSeek-6.7B degrades ${\sim}$12\pp{} for \cls{} and ${\sim}$25\pp{} for \cwm{}. The \cwm{} degradation is consistently larger than \cls{} in both families (4--5$\times$ for Qwen3, ${\sim}$2$\times$ for DeepSeek); the generation bottleneck at repo-level complexity is formulation-inherent. The absolute magnitude, however, is model-contingent: DeepSeek shows larger \cls{} degradation (${\sim}$12\pp{} vs.\ ${\sim}$5\pp{}), likely due to model-specific factors such as vocabulary size and tokenizer coverage for repository-level code. The \cwm{} numbers will be updated with final values upon training completion.
165	
166	\paragraph{Default assignment artifact.} The apparent \cwm{} ``improvement'' from 4B (58.04\%) to 8B (65.17\%) is an artifact of the default assignment protocol: unparseable outputs receive the majority-class label (pass, 72.96\% of the test set). As unparseable rate \emph{increases} from 53.7\% to 73.8\%, more predictions are replaced by this high-accuracy default, inflating overall accuracy. Parseable-only accuracy \emph{decreases} from 42.88\% (4B) to 38.52\% (8B): the generation model's actual predictive quality degrades with scale at this complexity level.
167	
168	\paragraph{Generation quality analysis.} Per-mutation analysis of \cwm{} 8B reveals degradation across all categories relative to 4B. The gold mutation (unmodified code) is most striking: parse rate collapses from 43.5\% (4B) to 23.9\% (8B), with parseable accuracy at 16.5\%. Even the simplest generation target (reproducing expected outputs for correct code) becomes harder at scale, consistent with a generation bottleneck that cross-model results suggest is formulation-inherent in direction but model-contingent in magnitude (\S\ref{sec:repo}, Cross-model validation). Detailed per-mutation breakdown appears in Appendix~\ref{app:repo_mutation}.
169	
170	\paragraph{Checkpoint sensitivity.} %%SCENARIO-A%% We tested for overtraining and found no evidence: at matched evaluation settings (\texttt{max\_seq\_length}=2048), epoch 2 and epoch 3 achieve comparable accuracy (${\sim}$65\%, $<$2\pp{} difference). The apparent ${\sim}$10\pp{} epoch degradation reported in earlier analysis was an artifact of inconsistent sequence length settings across evaluation runs (Evaluation sensitivity, below). This null result strengthens our causal attribution: \cwm{}'s repo-level failure is concentrated in format non-compliance (54--74\% unparseable outputs) rather than training instability.
171	
172	\paragraph{Evaluation sensitivity.} The same \cwm{} checkpoint evaluated at different \texttt{max\_seq\_length} settings produces ${\sim}$9\pp{} accuracy variation (\texttt{seq2048}: ${\sim}$65\% vs \texttt{seq4096}: ${\sim}$56\%), driven entirely by the unparseable rate $\to$ majority-class default chain: higher sequence length $\to$ lower unparseable rate $\to$ less majority-class inflation $\to$ lower apparent accuracy. This sensitivity is an inherent property of generation-based formulations with high format non-compliance: overall accuracy encodes evaluation protocol side-effects rather than pure predictive quality. Practitioners adopting \cwm{}-style formulations must standardize evaluation sequence length to obtain comparable results across studies. \cls{}, by contrast, produces a single token and is invariant to this parameter. %%END-SCENARIO-A%%
173	
174	\paragraph{Complexity as moderator.} The function-level \cls{}--\cwm{} gap widens from 1.32\pp{} (4B) to 2.62\pp{} (8B, $p = 0.0066$) with controlled methodology (matched seeds, same precision, no default assignment). At repo level, absolute gap magnitude (19--26\pp{}) is an order of magnitude larger than at function level (1--3\pp{}). This confirms complexity as the primary moderator of formulation divergence, though the repo-level data is from Qwen3 only.
175	
176	\paragraph{Sequence length.} A matched-context ablation confirms input context disparity explains $<$3\% of the \cls{}--\cwm{} gap: \cls{} 8B evaluated at \texttt{max\_seq\_length}$=$2,048 yields a matched-context gap of 25.00\pp{}, representing 98\% of the same-size 25.59\pp{} gap (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}).
177	
178	\paragraph{Limitations.} The same-size 4B comparison uses a single seed; \cls{} 4B seed 123 (val-best ckpt-800) achieves only 75.33\% test accuracy (+2.4\pp{} above majority baseline); val-best selection at an early checkpoint (step 800) amplifies the val-test distribution shift observed across all configurations. The \cwm{} 8B comparison is also single-seed. The validation and test sets have zero problem overlap but differ in mutation type composition (Appendix~\ref{app:mutation_distribution}): \texttt{operator\_swap} shifts from 19.1\% (val) to 34.3\% (test), the largest single-type distribution mismatch (+15.2\pp{}). All repo-level results use val-selected checkpoints, and checkpoint selection variance could contribute to observed differences. Our training data is synthetically constructed from mutation operators rather than real developer patches; the synthetic-to-real transfer gap remains untested. The \traj{} training configuration differs from \cls{}/\cwm{}: smaller training set (${\sim}$400 vs.\ ${\sim}$70K instances), different learning rate ($2{\times}10^{-4}$ vs.\ $5{\times}10^{-5}$), and variant-level evaluation ($n{=}48$ vs.\ per-test $n{=}9{,}784$). The function-level TOST equivalence (Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) is computed on matched test sets (1,032 variants) and remains valid. Repo-level \cls{}--\traj{} comparison is preliminary due to these configuration and granularity differences.
179	
180	
181	\subsection{Qualitative Error Analysis}
182	\label{sec:qualitative}
183	
184	To complement the quantitative divergence reported above, we examine representative errors from each formulation at repository level.
185	
186	\paragraph{\cwm{} failure modes.}
187	Two patterns account for the majority of unparseable \cwm{} outputs on Qwen3. (1)~\emph{Task misunderstanding}: the model generates a complete simulated test runner log (test names, timing, summary lines) rather than a pass/fail verdict. For instance, on \texttt{django\_\_django-14315}, the model outputs a fabricated 1,248-token Django test execution trace with hallucinated test names that do not exist in the target file. The model confuses ``predict test outcome'' with ``simulate test execution environment.'' (2)~\emph{Autoregressive degeneration}: the model enters a repetitive loop (e.g., repeating \texttt{from \_pytest.config import \_parse\_pytest\_args} until truncation at 4,542 tokens). Both patterns produce zero usable signal and default to the majority-class label. This explains why unparseable predictions carry higher accuracy than parseable ones (\S\ref{sec:repo}).
188	
189	\paragraph{\cls{} failure modes.}
190	\cls{} errors concentrate in two categories requiring multi-step reasoning. False positives arise from \emph{cross-concern value propagation}: a boundary removal mutation in \texttt{pytest} converts a guarded attribute access (\texttt{if xfailed and not xfailed.run}) to \texttt{if True:}, causing a NoneType error on tests exercising unrelated skip marks. The model fails to trace the None path through the pytest hook lifecycle. False negatives arise from \emph{mathematical equivalence}: on \texttt{HumanEval/130} (Tribonacci variant), the model predicts failure for correct code because verifying the self-referential recurrence requires algebraic substitution rather than syntactic inspection. This single problem accounts for 17\% of all function-level false negatives.
191	
192	\paragraph{Complementarity.}
193	These failure patterns are structurally orthogonal: \cwm{} fails at the format level (producing no usable signal), while \cls{} fails at the reasoning level (producing a confident but incorrect binary verdict). This qualitative observation corroborates the quantitative error complementarity (\S\ref{sec:analysis}, paragraph a), where an oracle combination of both formulations achieves 90.63\%.
194	
195	
196	\subsection{Analysis}
197	\label{sec:analysis}
198	
199	\paragraph{(a) Error complementarity.}
200	\cls{} and \cwm{} make errors in opposite directions at function level. Among \cls{} errors (seed 42), 75.5\% are false negatives (FAIL $\to$ PASS), meaning the classifier over-predicts passing. Among \cwm{} errors, 83.1\% are false positives (PASS $\to$ FAIL): string-matching failures incorrectly reject correct outputs. An oracle combination achieves 90.63\% accuracy, +3.12\pp{} over the best single formulation (87.51\%). This systematic complementarity indicates that the formulations capture different aspects of code behavior: classification encodes discriminative features directly, while generation must additionally model output structure. The result is orthogonal failure patterns. Ensemble approaches combining both signals are a natural direction.
201	
202	\paragraph{(b) CWM error mode breakdown.}
203	Among unparseable \cwm{} outputs (Qwen3, repo-level), 76.9\% contain no execution keywords; 22.9\% contain ambiguous co-occurring pass/fail keywords. Format compliance varies sharply across repositories (scikit-learn: near-zero unparseable; sphinx: 100\%); repository-specific code patterns interact with the model's generation capabilities in complex ways. This per-repository variation is consistent with a model/tokenizer-specific failure rather than a formulation-inherent one.
204	
205	\paragraph{(c) Relaxed matching does not rescue \cwm{}.}
206	Relaxed matching (tolerating format deviations, L0--L4) \emph{decreases} \cwm{} accuracy: 58.04\%$\to$56.98\% (seed 42) and 59.09\%$\to$55.91\% (seed 123). Unparseable predictions default to the majority class (pass, 72.96\%), which outperforms the model's own parseable predictions (42--53\%). The divergence widens from 26\pp{} to 28\pp{} under relaxed matching. This rules out the hypothesis that parsing strictness inflates the observed gap.
207	
208	\paragraph{(d) Variance compression.}
209	\cwm{}'s 5-seed standard deviation (0.37\pp{}) is lower than \cls{} (0.78\pp{}) and \traj{} (0.97\pp{}) at function level. If \cwm{} performance is capped by output-format constraints at a given complexity level, seed-to-seed variability is compressed relative to formulations whose performance depends more on learned representations.
210	
211	\paragraph{(e) Per-test precision and recall.}
212	\cls{} achieves fail \fone{} 87.86\% (precision 82.75\%, recall 93.64\%) at seed 42, with 42.1\% of problems perfectly predicted. Multi-F2P instances (6--10 tests) reach 96.3\% accuracy, consistent with the oracle finding that per-test granularity is most valuable for complex instances.
213	
214	\paragraph{(k) Aggregation penalty.} Per-test classification achieves 84.48\% per-test accuracy at repo level (Qwen3-8B, seed 42), but variant-level accuracy under the all-pass rule drops to 72.92\% (35/48 variants). The all-pass rule requires \emph{every} test in a variant to be correctly predicted; a single false negative flips the variant verdict. For variants with hundreds of tests (e.g., \texttt{astropy\_\_astropy-7336}: 357 tests, 2 false negatives $\to$ incorrect variant), even 99.4\% per-test accuracy produces a wrong variant-level prediction. This aggregation penalty is predictable from test count: function-level variants ($\bar{T} \approx 2.3$) incur negligible penalty (per-test $\approx$ variant-level), while repo-level variants ($\bar{T} \gg 10$) incur substantial penalty. The finding connects to the oracle analysis (\S\ref{sec:analysis}(a)): per-test granularity concentrates its \emph{advantage} at $T \geq 2$ but also its \emph{cost} at high $T$.
215	
216	\paragraph{(f) Block correlation.}
217	Within-problem errors are correlated (ICC $= 0.191$, DEFF $= 3.97$, 2.81$\times$ more all-correct problems than i.i.d.\ expectation). All statistical tests use clustered bootstrap accordingly.
218	
219	\paragraph{(g) LoRA rank and majority voting.}
220	Doubling adapter capacity (rank 32) does not improve \cwm{} accuracy ($-$0.51\pp{}, within seed variance); majority voting ($K{=}3$) decreases accuracy ($-$0.68\pp{}). Both interventions fail to resolve the generation bottleneck on Qwen3, consistent with the failure being formulation-level or model-specific rather than capacity-limited (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}).
221	
222	\paragraph{(h) Per-mutation scaling patterns.}
223	Scaling benefits are highly non-uniform across mutation types (Appendix, Table~\ref{tab:mutation_breakdown}). \texttt{off\_by\_one} shows the largest \cls{} scaling gain: 58.5\% (4B) to 86.9\% (8B, $+$28.4\pp{}). In contrast, \texttt{boundary\_removal} is scale-invariant at ${\sim}$47\% across both sizes, which marks an intrinsic difficulty ceiling. For \cwm{}, gold mutation parse rate collapses from 43.5\% (4B) to 23.9\% (8B) despite being the simplest target: generation capability degrades even for trivially correct code at larger scale within Qwen3. The val-test mutation type distribution differs notably (\texttt{operator\_swap} shifts from 19.1\% to 34.3\%; Appendix, Table~\ref{tab:mutation_distribution}), but this shift does not systematically bias overall accuracy because \cls{} performance on \texttt{operator\_swap} (78.9--81.4\%) is close to the overall mean.
224	
225	\paragraph{(i) Inference efficiency.}
226	\cls{} achieves 217$\times$ higher throughput than \cwm{} at repo level (853 vs.\ 3.9 inferences/min; Qwen3-4B, RTX 5090, 100 samples, avg ${\sim}$1,040 input tokens). The speedup derives directly from the formulation: classification requires a single output token, whereas \cwm{} generates an average of 477 tokens per inference. Median latency is 48ms (\cls{}) vs.\ 16.3s (\cwm{}). At the observed unparseable rate of 73.8\% (8B), the majority of \cwm{}'s generation budget produces tokens that cannot yield a valid verdict. This \cls{}--\cwm{} efficiency gap is an inherent property of the formulation difference (1 vs.\ $O(L)$ output tokens) and should generalize across models, unlike the accuracy divergence which may be model-contingent.
227	
228	The \cls{}--\traj{} efficiency comparison is more nuanced, because \cls{} makes one call per test while \traj{} makes one call per instance. Per-call, \cls{} is 20--138$\times$ faster (depending on input length: 47ms for short contexts vs.\ 2,632ms for \traj{}). However, the per-instance effective speedup depends on test count $|T|$: at function level ($|T| \approx 2.3$), \cls{} is ${\sim}$24$\times$ faster (108ms vs.\ 2,632ms per instance); at repo level ($|T| \approx 12.3$, ${\sim}$70ms/call with longer inputs), \cls{} is ${\sim}$3$\times$ faster (861ms vs.\ 2,631ms). A crossover exists at $|T| \approx 50$ tests, beyond which \traj{}'s single-call amortization dominates. This boundary condition is informative for deployment: \cls{} is strongly preferred for function-level and moderate repo-level test suites, while extremely large test suites ($>$50 tests per instance) favor trajectory-level efficiency despite its coarser diagnostic signal.
229	
230	\paragraph{(j) Re-repair pilot.}
231	We tested whether per-test diagnostic feedback improves downstream repair. A Qwen3-8B repair agent received either per-test diagnostic signals (which specific tests failed and why) or binary accept/reject feedback, evaluated on 44 SWE-bench Verified instances. The diagnostic condition achieved accuracy no higher than the binary baseline ($A_1 = 52.3\%$, $p = 0.42$, effect size $\approx 0$). Per-test diagnostic information does not automatically translate to repair improvements at this scale. The negative result may reflect insufficient agent capability to exploit fine-grained feedback, insufficient sample size (at $n = 44$, statistical power is ${\sim}$0.3 for a medium effect size), or a genuine limitation of the diagnostic-to-repair translation.
232	
```

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe systematically compare \\cls{}, \\cwm{}, and \\traj{} across two model families, two complexity levels, and multiple seeds to characterize the formulation design space for neural code verification.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.}\n\\emph{Function-level}: mutated variants from HumanEval~\\cite{chen2021humaneval} and MBPP~\\cite{austin2021mbpp} (five mutation types), labels verified by execution. 19,076 samples (train 15,290 / val 1,896 / test 1,890; pass:fail $\\approx$ 52:48, majority baseline 51.75\\%).\n\\emph{Repo-level}: per-test instances from SWE-bench Verified~\\cite{chowdhury2024swebenchverified} mutants. ${\\sim}$70K training samples; val ($n{=}9{,}335$) and test ($n{=}9{,}784$) have zero problem overlap (majority baseline 72.96\\%). Val-based checkpoint selection follows standard practice~\\cite{jimenez2024swebench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Models and training.}\nWe fine-tune Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B~\\cite{qwen2025qwen3} with LoRA~\\cite{hu2022lora} (rank 16, 3 epochs) across five seeds. Seeds 42/123/456 use bfloat16; seeds 789/0 use NF4 quantization~\\cite{dettmers2024qlora}. For cross-model validation, we additionally fine-tune DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B~\\cite{guo2024deepseekcoder} with identical LoRA configuration (2 seeds each for \\cls{} and \\cwm{}). Our \\cwm{} baseline is a LoRA fine-tuned variant for controlled comparison, not a reproduction of the 32B Code World Model~\\cite{meta2025cwm}. Repo-level models use val-selected checkpoints. Zero-shot baselines use Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-32B (NF4) as LLM-as-judge. Full hyperparameter details appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:hyperparams}.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistics.}\nConfidence intervals use clustered bootstrap~\\cite{davison1997bootstrap} (114 problem clusters, 10K resamples); per-test vs.\\ trajectory-level comparison uses TOST~\\cite{schuirmann1987tost} ($\\pm$2\\pp{} margin). ICC~\\cite{shrout1979icc} quantifies within-problem correlation. Oracle analysis uses SWE-bench Verified (500 instances).\n\n\n\\subsection{Function-Level: Formulation Equivalence}\n\\label{sec:func_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports function-level results across formulations and model families.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Function-level per-test verification results on HumanEval+MBPP test set. \\cls{}: per-test binary classification; \\cwm{}: execution output generation; \\traj{}: trajectory-level scoring; ZS: zero-shot LLM-as-judge. Mean $\\pm$ std over seeds reported where applicable. Seeds 42/123/456 use full bfloat16; seeds 789/0 use NF4 quantization. \\cls{} and \\cwm{} are evaluated on the full 1,890 test samples; \\traj{} is evaluated on a 1,032-sample subset covering the same 447 (problem, mutation) variants with representative test cases selected to fit the trajectory input format.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Seeds} & \\textbf{Accuracy (\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\emph{Qwen3 (primary)}} \\\\\n\\cls{} (fine-tuned) & 4B & 5 & \\textbf{88.43 $\\pm$ 0.78} \\\\\n\\cwm{} (fine-tuned) & 4B & 5 & 87.19 $\\pm$ 0.37 \\\\\n\\traj{} (fine-tuned) & 4B & 5 & 86.30 $\\pm$ 0.97 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\emph{DeepSeek-Coder (cross-model validation)}} \\\\\n\\cls{} (fine-tuned) & 6.7B & 2 & 86.85 $\\pm$ 0.11 \\\\\n\\cwm{} (fine-tuned) & 6.7B & 2 & 86.83 $\\pm$ 1.49 \\\\\n\\traj{} (fine-tuned) & 6.7B & 2 & 84.16 $\\pm$ 1.30 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\emph{Prompting baselines}} \\\\\n0-shot & 8B (Qwen3) & 1 & 68.70 \\\\\n0-shot & 6.7B (DeepSeek) & 1 & 63.00 \\\\\nZS LLM-as-judge & 8B (Qwen3) & 1 & 64.40 \\\\\nZS LLM-as-judge & 6.7B (DeepSeek) & 1 & 56.56 \\\\\nZS LLM-as-judge & 32B (NF4) & 1 & 71.69 \\\\\n3-shot ICL & 8B (Qwen3) & 1 & 68.78 \\\\\n3-shot ICL & 6.7B (DeepSeek) & 1 & 59.95 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority (pass) & --- & --- & 51.75 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll three formulations achieve comparable accuracy on Qwen3-4B: \\cls{} 88.43\\% ($\\pm$0.78), \\cwm{} 87.19\\% ($\\pm$0.37), \\traj{} 86.30\\% ($\\pm$0.97). The \\cls{}--\\cwm{} gap of 1.24\\pp{} is modest, within 1.6$\\times$ of the seed standard deviation for \\cls{}. DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B confirms this pattern across all three formulations: \\cls{} 86.85\\% ($\\pm$0.11), \\cwm{} 86.83\\% ($\\pm$1.49), \\traj{} 84.16\\% ($\\pm$1.30). The \\cls{}--\\cwm{} gap of 0.02\\pp{} is indistinguishable from zero; \\traj{} is 2.69\\pp{} below \\cls{}, consistent with the Qwen3 ordering. At this complexity level, execution outputs are short and structured, generation-specific failure modes (truncation, format compliance) are not triggered, and formulation choice does not meaningfully affect accuracy. Under standard instruction-following prompting, baselines reveal three layers of the verification challenge. First, \\emph{repo-level inherent difficulty}: 0-shot accuracy drops to 49.9\\% (Qwen3-8B) and 48.5\\% (DeepSeek-6.7B, 8.7\\% unparseable) at repo level, below the majority baseline (72.96\\%). Both models exhibit systematic but non-discriminative bias (Qwen3 predicts fail 75\\% of the time; DeepSeek predicts pass 73\\%), achieving ${\\sim}$49\\% accuracy in both cases. Three-shot ICL fails entirely at repo level (100\\% unparseable due to context overflow). Second, \\emph{fine-tuning necessity}: the 0-shot$\\to$fine-tuned gain is larger at repo level (25--35\\pp{}) than at function level (18--20\\pp{}); repository-level verification requires task-specific training under standard prompting, not merely a capable base model. At function level, 0-shot achieves 68.70\\% (Qwen3-8B) and 63.00\\% (DeepSeek-6.7B); the LLM-as-judge variant is lower (64.40\\%, 56.56\\%); 3-shot ICL reaches 68.78\\% and 59.95\\%. Third, \\emph{formulation choice}: even after fine-tuning, formulation selection matters at repo level (\\cls{} outperforms \\cwm{} by 13--26\\pp{}), an interaction effect absent at function level where all formulations converge. \\cwm{} produces 0\\% unparseable outputs at function level for both model families.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance asymmetry.} \\cwm{}'s higher seed variance on DeepSeek ($\\sigma{=}1.49$ vs.\\ \\cls{} $\\sigma{=}0.11$) mirrors the Qwen3 pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}, paragraph d): generation is more sensitive to initialization than classification, consistent with the output space being less constrained.\n\n\n\\subsection{Scaling Behavior}\n\\label{sec:scaling}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scaling} compares all three formulations at two model sizes using two matched seeds (42, 123), all trained in full bfloat16 precision to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Model-size scaling: accuracy (\\%) at 4B and 8B (Qwen3, two matched seeds in full precision). \\cls{} and \\traj{} scale positively; \\cwm{} does not.}\n\\label{tab:scaling}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Size} & \\textbf{s42} & \\textbf{s123} & \\textbf{Mean} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ 4B$\\to$8B} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\cls{} & 4B & 87.83 & 89.52 & 88.68 & \\multirow{2}{*}{+1.27} \\\\\n\\cls{} & 8B & \\textbf{90.48} & 89.40 & \\textbf{89.94} & \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\cwm{} & 4B & 87.51 & 87.20 & 87.36 & \\multirow{2}{*}{$-$0.03} \\\\\n\\cwm{} & 8B & 87.67 & 86.98 & 87.33 & \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\traj{} & 4B & 85.27 & 86.72 & 86.00 & \\multirow{2}{*}{+1.70} \\\\\n\\traj{} & 8B & 87.60 & 87.79 & 87.70 & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\cls{} and \\traj{} both scale positively from 4B to 8B (+1.27\\pp{} and +1.70\\pp{} respectively), while \\cwm{} remains flat ($-$0.03\\pp{}). At 8B, the ranking is \\cls{} (89.94) $>$ \\traj{} (87.70) $>$ \\cwm{} (87.33). The \\cls{}--\\cwm{} gap widens from 1.32\\pp{} at 4B to 2.62\\pp{} at 8B (Figure~\\ref{fig:scaling_gap}); clustered bootstrap on the 3-seed superset (42, 123, 456 for \\cls{}/\\cwm{}) yields $p = 0.0066$ (95\\% CI $[1.80, 2.71]$), which confirms the widening is not a sampling artifact.\n\n\\cwm{} is the only formulation that does not benefit from increased capacity under our experimental configuration (LoRA rank 16, fixed hyperparameters). The \\cwm{} stagnation ($-$0.03\\pp{}) falls within seed noise and could reflect suboptimal hyperparameter transfer at 8B rather than a fundamental scaling limitation. We do not claim that generation \\emph{cannot} benefit from scale, only that it did not in this configuration. This pattern is observed within Qwen3 only; whether it generalizes across model families at function level is not yet tested. At repository-level complexity, the interaction between formulation and scale is examined in \\S\\ref{sec:repo}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Per-Test vs.\\ Trajectory-Level Equivalence}\n\\label{sec:equivalence}\n\nWe compare \\cls{} (aggregated via all-pass rule) against \\traj{} on 523 variant-level instances across five seeds. The pooled gap is $-$0.45\\pp{} (95\\% CI [$-$1.71, +0.84]); the TOST equivalence test confirms statistical equivalence at $p = 0.007$ ($\\pm$2\\pp{} margin), with a minimum equivalence margin of $\\pm$1.63\\pp{}. The $\\pm$2\\pp{} margin reflects practical significance: formulation differences below 2\\pp{} are unlikely to affect deployment decisions where absolute accuracy dominates the cost-benefit analysis. Within-problem predictions are correlated (ICC $= 0.191$, $p < 0.0001$, DEFF $= 3.97$); all statistical tests use clustered bootstrap accordingly. On DeepSeek-Coder, the observed \\cls{}--\\cwm{} gap of 0.02\\pp{} is consistent with equivalence, but with only 2 seeds the comparison is underpowered (TOST not significant even at $\\pm$5\\pp{} margin). Per-test classification provides diagnostic information (which specific tests fail) at no cost to variant-level accuracy, a result that parallels process supervision~\\cite{lightman2023letsverify}, where step-level verification enables error localization without accuracy cost.\n\n\\paragraph{TOST margin sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports equivalence test results across multiple margins. The observed mean difference is 1.24\\pp{} (\\cls{} $>$ \\cwm{}), so the minimum margin at which equivalence holds is $\\pm$1.63\\pp{}. At $\\pm$1\\pp{}, the margin is too tight to contain the observed difference ($p = 0.871$); at $\\pm$2\\pp{}, equivalence is confirmed ($p = 0.007$); at $\\pm$3\\pp{}, evidence is overwhelming ($p < 0.001$). We adopt $\\pm$2\\pp{} as the primary margin because formulation differences below 2\\pp{} are unlikely to affect deployment decisions: at this scale, factors such as seed selection, checkpoint choice, and hyperparameter tuning dominate the accuracy variance.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{TOST equivalence sensitivity (Qwen3-4B, 5 seeds, $\\alpha{=}0.05$). Observed mean \\cls{}--\\cwm{} difference: 1.24\\pp{}.}\n\\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{ccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Margin $\\delta$ (\\pp{})} & \\textbf{TOST $p$} & \\textbf{Equivalent?} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\pm$1 & 0.871 & No \\\\\n$\\pm$2 & 0.007 & Yes \\\\\n$\\pm$3 & $<$0.001 & Yes \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{\\cls{}--\\traj{} accuracy relationship.}\nAcross both model families, \\cls{} achieves slightly higher accuracy than \\traj{}: Qwen3-4B \\cls{} 88.43\\% vs.\\ \\traj{} 86.30\\% (gap 2.13\\pp{}); DeepSeek-6.7B \\cls{} 86.85\\% vs.\\ \\traj{} 84.16\\% (gap 2.69\\pp{}). The consistency of this ordering across models suggests that per-test decomposition provides a modest accuracy benefit beyond diagnostic granularity: processing each test independently allows the model to focus on a single pass/fail decision rather than integrating signals across an entire test trajectory. \\cls{} additionally offers ${\\sim}$24$\\times$ per-instance speedup over \\traj{} at function level (2.3 tests/instance $\\times$ 47ms/call vs.\\ 2,632ms single \\traj{} call; DeepSeek-6.7B, RTX 5090). This advantage narrows at repo level (${\\sim}$3$\\times$; 12.3 tests $\\times$ 70ms vs.\\ 2,631ms) because \\cls{} per-test cost grows linearly with test count. Combined with the diagnostic granularity advantage (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle-results}), per-test classification Pareto-dominates trajectory-level scoring on accuracy, efficiency, and informativeness at function-level complexity; at repo level the efficiency advantage is modest and disappears entirely for instances with $>$50 tests.\n\n\n\\subsection{Accuracy-Utility Frontier}\n\\label{sec:oracle-results}\n\nOracle analysis on SWE-bench Verified (500 instances) reveals that per-test advantage concentrates sharply: at $T = 1$ F2P tests (68.7\\% of instances), per-test and trajectory-level are informationally equivalent. The advantage materializes at $T \\geq 2$ (31.3\\%), with $T = 2$ alone contributing 55.3\\% of the total 10.93\\pp{} block-correlated advantage. Pass-to-pass (P2P) regression is negligible (1.04\\% mean, 0\\% median), so the practical value concentrates in fail-to-pass discrimination. Full stratification and the i.i.d.\\ vs.\\ block-correlated comparison appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Repo-Level: Complexity-Dependent Divergence}\n\\label{sec:repo}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:repo_level} compares \\cls{} and \\cwm{} on SWE-bench Verified per-test classification at repository-level complexity. All checkpoints are selected by validation accuracy; the test set ($n{=}9{,}784$) has zero problem overlap with validation. We report both overall accuracy (where unparseable outputs default to the majority class, pass) and parseable-only accuracy to disentangle generation quality from default assignment effects.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Repo-level cross-formulation comparison on SWE-bench Verified test set ($n{=}9{,}784$). \\cwm{} unparseable outputs default to pass (majority class). Seeds: Qwen3 primary seed 42; DeepSeek \\traj{} 6 seeds. \\textsuperscript{$\\star$}\\cwm{} preliminary (training in progress).}\n\\label{tab:repo_level}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{\\cls{} Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{\\cwm{} Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{\\cwm{} Unparse (\\%)} & \\textbf{\\traj{} Acc (\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-4B & \\textbf{83.63} & 58.04 & 53.70 & TBD \\\\\nQwen3-8B & 84.48 / 85.60\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} & 65.17 & 73.80 & TBD \\\\\nDeepSeek-6.7B & 74.93 & ${\\sim}$60\\textsuperscript{$\\star$} & TBD & 75.35 $\\pm$ 5.00 \\\\\n\\midrule\n0-shot (Qwen3-8B) & 49.90 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\n0-shot (DeepSeek-6.7B) & 48.50 & --- & 8.70 & --- \\\\\nMajority baseline & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{72.96} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$}Two seeds (42 / 123). \\textsuperscript{$\\star$}\\cwm{} running accuracy; final values pending. \\cwm{} parseable-only accuracy: 42.88\\% (4B), 38.52\\% (8B).}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Formulation divergence.} At repository-level complexity, \\cls{} and \\cwm{} diverge sharply on Qwen3: the gap is 25.59\\pp{} at 4B (\\cls{} 83.63\\% vs.\\ \\cwm{} 58.04\\%, seed 42; Figure~\\ref{fig:scaling_gap}) and 19.31\\pp{} at 8B (84.48\\% vs.\\ 65.17\\%). Both gaps are an order of magnitude larger than at function level (1--3\\pp{}, Table~\\ref{tab:scaling}); formulation effectiveness is complexity-dependent. \\cwm{} falls below the majority baseline at both scales ($-$14.92\\pp{} at 4B, $-$7.79\\pp{} at 8B): negative utility for this model/formulation combination.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.} DeepSeek-Coder repo-level results (\\cls{} final, \\cwm{} preliminary) confirm the formulation-inherent nature of the divergence while revealing model-contingent magnitude. Both model families exhibit the same ordering (\\cls{} $>$ \\cwm{}), but the function$\\to$repo degradation differs: Qwen3-8B degrades ${\\sim}$5\\pp{} for \\cls{} and ${\\sim}$22\\pp{} for \\cwm{}, while DeepSeek-6.7B degrades ${\\sim}$12\\pp{} for \\cls{} and ${\\sim}$25\\pp{} for \\cwm{}. The \\cwm{} degradation is consistently larger than \\cls{} in both families (4--5$\\times$ for Qwen3, ${\\sim}$2$\\times$ for DeepSeek); the generation bottleneck at repo-level complexity is formulation-inherent. The absolute magnitude, however, is model-contingent: DeepSeek shows larger \\cls{} degradation (${\\sim}$12\\pp{} vs.\\ ${\\sim}$5\\pp{}), likely due to model-specific factors such as vocabulary size and tokenizer coverage for repository-level code. The \\cwm{} numbers will be updated with final values upon training completion.\n\n\\paragraph{Default assignment artifact.} The apparent \\cwm{} ``improvement'' from 4B (58.04\\%) to 8B (65.17\\%) is an artifact of the default assignment protocol: unparseable outputs receive the majority-class label (pass, 72.96\\% of the test set). As unparseable rate \\emph{increases} from 53.7\\% to 73.8\\%, more predictions are replaced by this high-accuracy default, inflating overall accuracy. Parseable-only accuracy \\emph{decreases} from 42.88\\% (4B) to 38.52\\% (8B): the generation model's actual predictive quality degrades with scale at this complexity level.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation quality analysis.} Per-mutation analysis of \\cwm{} 8B reveals degradation across all categories relative to 4B. The gold mutation (unmodified code) is most striking: parse rate collapses from 43.5\\% (4B) to 23.9\\% (8B), with parseable accuracy at 16.5\\%. Even the simplest generation target (reproducing expected outputs for correct code) becomes harder at scale, consistent with a generation bottleneck that cross-model results suggest is formulation-inherent in direction but model-contingent in magnitude (\\S\\ref{sec:repo}, Cross-model validation). Detailed per-mutation breakdown appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:repo_mutation}.\n\n\\paragraph{Checkpoint sensitivity.} %%SCENARIO-A%% We tested for overtraining and found no evidence: at matched evaluation settings (\\texttt{max\\_seq\\_length}=2048), epoch 2 and epoch 3 achieve comparable accuracy (${\\sim}$65\\%, $<$2\\pp{} difference). The apparent ${\\sim}$10\\pp{} epoch degradation reported in earlier analysis was an artifact of inconsistent sequence length settings across evaluation runs (Evaluation sensitivity, below). This null result strengthens our causal attribution: \\cwm{}'s repo-level failure is concentrated in format non-compliance (54--74\\% unparseable outputs) rather than training instability.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation sensitivity.} The same \\cwm{} checkpoint evaluated at different \\texttt{max\\_seq\\_length} settings produces ${\\sim}$9\\pp{} accuracy variation (\\texttt{seq2048}: ${\\sim}$65\\% vs \\texttt{seq4096}: ${\\sim}$56\\%), driven entirely by the unparseable rate $\\to$ majority-class default chain: higher sequence length $\\to$ lower unparseable rate $\\to$ less majority-class inflation $\\to$ lower apparent accuracy. This sensitivity is an inherent property of generation-based formulations with high format non-compliance: overall accuracy encodes evaluation protocol side-effects rather than pure predictive quality. Practitioners adopting \\cwm{}-style formulations must standardize evaluation sequence length to obtain comparable results across studies. \\cls{}, by contrast, produces a single token and is invariant to this parameter. %%END-SCENARIO-A%%\n\n\\paragraph{Complexity as moderator.} The function-level \\cls{}--\\cwm{} gap widens from 1.32\\pp{} (4B) to 2.62\\pp{} (8B, $p = 0.0066$) with controlled methodology (matched seeds, same precision, no default assignment). At repo level, absolute gap magnitude (19--26\\pp{}) is an order of magnitude larger than at function level (1--3\\pp{}). This confirms complexity as the primary moderator of formulation divergence, though the repo-level data is from Qwen3 only.\n\n\\paragraph{Sequence length.} A matched-context ablation confirms input context disparity explains $<$3\\% of the \\cls{}--\\cwm{} gap: \\cls{} 8B evaluated at \\texttt{max\\_seq\\_length}$=$2,048 yields a matched-context gap of 25.00\\pp{}, representing 98\\% of the same-size 25.59\\pp{} gap (Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} The same-size 4B comparison uses a single seed; \\cls{} 4B seed 123 (val-best ckpt-800) achieves only 75.33\\% test accuracy (+2.4\\pp{} above majority baseline); val-best selection at an early checkpoint (step 800) amplifies the val-test distribution shift observed across all configurations. The \\cwm{} 8B comparison is also single-seed. The validation and test sets have zero problem overlap but differ in mutation type composition (Appendix~\\ref{app:mutation_distribution}): \\texttt{operator\\_swap} shifts from 19.1\\% (val) to 34.3\\% (test), the largest single-type distribution mismatch (+15.2\\pp{}). All repo-level results use val-selected checkpoints, and checkpoint selection variance could contribute to observed differences. Our training data is synthetically constructed from mutation operators rather than real developer patches; the synthetic-to-real transfer gap remains untested. The \\traj{} training configuration differs from \\cls{}/\\cwm{}: smaller training set (${\\sim}$400 vs.\\ ${\\sim}$70K instances), different learning rate ($2{\\times}10^{-4}$ vs.\\ $5{\\times}10^{-5}$), and variant-level evaluation ($n{=}48$ vs.\\ per-test $n{=}9{,}784$). The function-level TOST equivalence (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) is computed on matched test sets (1,032 variants) and remains valid. Repo-level \\cls{}--\\traj{} comparison is preliminary due to these configuration and granularity differences.\n\n\n\\subsection{Qualitative Error Analysis}\n\\label{sec:qualitative}\n\nTo complement the quantitative divergence reported above, we examine representative errors from each formulation at repository level.\n\n\\paragraph{\\cwm{} failure modes.}\nTwo patterns account for the majority of unparseable \\cwm{} outputs on Qwen3. (1)~\\emph{Task misunderstanding}: the model generates a complete simulated test runner log (test names, timing, summary lines) rather than a pass/fail verdict. For instance, on \\texttt{django\\_\\_django-14315}, the model outputs a fabricated 1,248-token Django test execution trace with hallucinated test names that do not exist in the target file. The model confuses ``predict test outcome'' with ``simulate test execution environment.'' (2)~\\emph{Autoregressive degeneration}: the model enters a repetitive loop (e.g., repeating \\texttt{from \\_pytest.config import \\_parse\\_pytest\\_args} until truncation at 4,542 tokens). Both patterns produce zero usable signal and default to the majority-class label. This explains why unparseable predictions carry higher accuracy than parseable ones (\\S\\ref{sec:repo}).\n\n\\paragraph{\\cls{} failure modes.}\n\\cls{} errors concentrate in two categories requiring multi-step reasoning. False positives arise from \\emph{cross-concern value propagation}: a boundary removal mutation in \\texttt{pytest} converts a guarded attribute access (\\texttt{if xfailed and not xfailed.run}) to \\texttt{if True:}, causing a NoneType error on tests exercising unrelated skip marks. The model fails to trace the None path through the pytest hook lifecycle. False negatives arise from \\emph{mathematical equivalence}: on \\texttt{HumanEval/130} (Tribonacci variant), the model predicts failure for correct code because verifying the self-referential recurrence requires algebraic substitution rather than syntactic inspection. This single problem accounts for 17\\% of all function-level false negatives.\n\n\\paragraph{Complementarity.}\nThese failure patterns are structurally orthogonal: \\cwm{} fails at the format level (producing no usable signal), while \\cls{} fails at the reasoning level (producing a confident but incorrect binary verdict). This qualitative observation corroborates the quantitative error complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}, paragraph a), where an oracle combination of both formulations achieves 90.63\\%.\n\n\n\\subsection{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{(a) Error complementarity.}\n\\cls{} and \\cwm{} make errors in opposite directions at function level. Among \\cls{} errors (seed 42), 75.5\\% are false negatives (FAIL $\\to$ PASS), meaning the classifier over-predicts passing. Among \\cwm{} errors, 83.1\\% are false positives (PASS $\\to$ FAIL): string-matching failures incorrectly reject correct outputs. An oracle combination achieves 90.63\\% accuracy, +3.12\\pp{} over the best single formulation (87.51\\%). This systematic complementarity indicates that the formulations capture different aspects of code behavior: classification encodes discriminative features directly, while generation must additionally model output structure. The result is orthogonal failure patterns. Ensemble approaches combining both signals are a natural direction.\n\n\\paragraph{(b) CWM error mode breakdown.}\nAmong unparseable \\cwm{} outputs (Qwen3, repo-level), 76.9\\% contain no execution keywords; 22.9\\% contain ambiguous co-occurring pass/fail keywords. Format compliance varies sharply across repositories (scikit-learn: near-zero unparseable; sphinx: 100\\%); repository-specific code patterns interact with the model's generation capabilities in complex ways. This per-repository variation is consistent with a model/tokenizer-specific failure rather than a formulation-inherent one.\n\n\\paragraph{(c) Relaxed matching does not rescue \\cwm{}.}\nRelaxed matching (tolerating format deviations, L0--L4) \\emph{decreases} \\cwm{} accuracy: 58.04\\%$\\to$56.98\\% (seed 42) and 59.09\\%$\\to$55.91\\% (seed 123). Unparseable predictions default to the majority class (pass, 72.96\\%), which outperforms the model's own parseable predictions (42--53\\%). The divergence widens from 26\\pp{} to 28\\pp{} under relaxed matching. This rules out the hypothesis that parsing strictness inflates the observed gap.\n\n\\paragraph{(d) Variance compression.}\n\\cwm{}'s 5-seed standard deviation (0.37\\pp{}) is lower than \\cls{} (0.78\\pp{}) and \\traj{} (0.97\\pp{}) at function level. If \\cwm{} performance is capped by output-format constraints at a given complexity level, seed-to-seed variability is compressed relative to formulations whose performance depends more on learned representations.\n\n\\paragraph{(e) Per-test precision and recall.}\n\\cls{} achieves fail \\fone{} 87.86\\% (precision 82.75\\%, recall 93.64\\%) at seed 42, with 42.1\\% of problems perfectly predicted. Multi-F2P instances (6--10 tests) reach 96.3\\% accuracy, consistent with the oracle finding that per-test granularity is most valuable for complex instances.\n\n\\paragraph{(k) Aggregation penalty.} Per-test classification achieves 84.48\\% per-test accuracy at repo level (Qwen3-8B, seed 42), but variant-level accuracy under the all-pass rule drops to 72.92\\% (35/48 variants). The all-pass rule requires \\emph{every} test in a variant to be correctly predicted; a single false negative flips the variant verdict. For variants with hundreds of tests (e.g., \\texttt{astropy\\_\\_astropy-7336}: 357 tests, 2 false negatives $\\to$ incorrect variant), even 99.4\\% per-test accuracy produces a wrong variant-level prediction. This aggregation penalty is predictable from test count: function-level variants ($\\bar{T} \\approx 2.3$) incur negligible penalty (per-test $\\approx$ variant-level), while repo-level variants ($\\bar{T} \\gg 10$) incur substantial penalty. The finding connects to the oracle analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}(a)): per-test granularity concentrates its \\emph{advantage} at $T \\geq 2$ but also its \\emph{cost} at high $T$.\n\n\\paragraph{(f) Block correlation.}\nWithin-problem errors are correlated (ICC $= 0.191$, DEFF $= 3.97$, 2.81$\\times$ more all-correct problems than i.i.d.\\ expectation). All statistical tests use clustered bootstrap accordingly.\n\n\\paragraph{(g) LoRA rank and majority voting.}\nDoubling adapter capacity (rank 32) does not improve \\cwm{} accuracy ($-$0.51\\pp{}, within seed variance); majority voting ($K{=}3$) decreases accuracy ($-$0.68\\pp{}). Both interventions fail to resolve the generation bottleneck on Qwen3, consistent with the failure being formulation-level or model-specific rather than capacity-limited (Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{(h) Per-mutation scaling patterns.}\nScaling benefits are highly non-uniform across mutation types (Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:mutation_breakdown}). \\texttt{off\\_by\\_one} shows the largest \\cls{} scaling gain: 58.5\\% (4B) to 86.9\\% (8B, $+$28.4\\pp{}). In contrast, \\texttt{boundary\\_removal} is scale-invariant at ${\\sim}$47\\% across both sizes, which marks an intrinsic difficulty ceiling. For \\cwm{}, gold mutation parse rate collapses from 43.5\\% (4B) to 23.9\\% (8B) despite being the simplest target: generation capability degrades even for trivially correct code at larger scale within Qwen3. The val-test mutation type distribution differs notably (\\texttt{operator\\_swap} shifts from 19.1\\% to 34.3\\%; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:mutation_distribution}), but this shift does not systematically bias overall accuracy because \\cls{} performance on \\texttt{operator\\_swap} (78.9--81.4\\%) is close to the overall mean.\n\n\\paragraph{(i) Inference efficiency.}\n\\cls{} achieves 217$\\times$ higher throughput than \\cwm{} at repo level (853 vs.\\ 3.9 inferences/min; Qwen3-4B, RTX 5090, 100 samples, avg ${\\sim}$1,040 input tokens). The speedup derives directly from the formulation: classification requires a single output token, whereas \\cwm{} generates an average of 477 tokens per inference. Median latency is 48ms (\\cls{}) vs.\\ 16.3s (\\cwm{}). At the observed unparseable rate of 73.8\\% (8B), the majority of \\cwm{}'s generation budget produces tokens that cannot yield a valid verdict. This \\cls{}--\\cwm{} efficiency gap is an inherent property of the formulation difference (1 vs.\\ $O(L)$ output tokens) and should generalize across models, unlike the accuracy divergence which may be model-contingent.\n\nThe \\cls{}--\\traj{} efficiency comparison is more nuanced, because \\cls{} makes one call per test while \\traj{} makes one call per instance. Per-call, \\cls{} is 20--138$\\times$ faster (depending on input length: 47ms for short contexts vs.\\ 2,632ms for \\traj{}). However, the per-instance effective speedup depends on test count $|T|$: at function level ($|T| \\approx 2.3$), \\cls{} is ${\\sim}$24$\\times$ faster (108ms vs.\\ 2,632ms per instance); at repo level ($|T| \\approx 12.3$, ${\\sim}$70ms/call with longer inputs), \\cls{} is ${\\sim}$3$\\times$ faster (861ms vs.\\ 2,631ms). A crossover exists at $|T| \\approx 50$ tests, beyond which \\traj{}'s single-call amortization dominates. This boundary condition is informative for deployment: \\cls{} is strongly preferred for function-level and moderate repo-level test suites, while extremely large test suites ($>$50 tests per instance) favor trajectory-level efficiency despite its coarser diagnostic signal.\n\n\\paragraph{(j) Re-repair pilot.}\nWe tested whether per-test diagnostic feedback improves downstream repair. A Qwen3-8B repair agent received either per-test diagnostic signals (which specific tests failed and why) or binary accept/reject feedback, evaluated on 44 SWE-bench Verified instances. The diagnostic condition achieved accuracy no higher than the binary baseline ($A_1 = 52.3\\%$, $p = 0.42$, effect size $\\approx 0$). Per-test diagnostic information does not automatically translate to repair improvements at this scale. The negative result may reflect insufficient agent capability to exploit fine-grained feedback, insufficient sample size (at $n = 44$, statistical power is ${\\sim}$0.3 for a medium effect size), or a genuine limitation of the diagnostic-to-repair translation.\n",
